I read the full texts of ROSDiscover, ROSInfer and three HAROS papers, plus the HAROS/ROSDiscover code. For ROS/ROS 2, DDS and cFE/cFS I read docs and source pinned to commits. I counted ROBUST concurrency bugs myself from the dataset. Most academic hosts were blocked (arXiv, ACM, IEEE, Dagstuhl, MPI-SWS, omg.org, autosar.org, docs.ros.org, NTRS, university sites). For those papers I have only search-index snippets, artifact code, or a third-party thesis summary, and each finding says which.\n\nMain results:\n(1) Venue correction: ROSDiscover is ICSA'22 (DOI 10.1109/ICSA53651.2022.00019), not ICSE'22. It recovers only topology and types: ports, parameters, launch-file remaps. It checks structural rules (type mismatch, dangling connectors). It checks no ordering or timing.\n(2) ROSInfer (ICSE'24) already infers, from C++ ROS source, component state machines. Subscriber callbacks set state variables, and those variables guard periodic or reactive publishes. It then model-checks the system in TLA+ to find components that wait forever for an input nobody sends. This is close to the original note's 'message -> state field -> guarded use' idea. The authors say their models cannot be used to analyse race conditions or timing, and they name NASA F Prime as a generalization target.\n(3) HAROS extracts the ROS computation graph with path conditions, loop flags, queue sizes and latched flags. Its HPL language can state ordering and real-time properties, but those are checked at runtime or by property-based testing. Its Electrum model checking drops real-time and assumes every subscriber is fixed from the start and eventually gets every message. So first-message loss and staleness cannot be expressed there.\n(4) ROS and DDS handle first-message loss and staleness in the framework: ROS 1 latch, ROS 2/DDS TRANSIENT_LOCAL durability with a compatibility rule, LIFESPAN, DEADLINE, matched events, message_filters timestamp synchronizers, and rclc LET sampling. ROSpec checks declared QoS compatibility statically.\n(5) At the pinned cFE commit 546a0025, SB QoS is 'currently unused' and I found no durability or latching concept. A transmit with no subscribers succeeds, increments a counter and sends an event. Subscribe and destination lookup both run under the SB shared-data mutex, and delivery order is reverse subscription order. My inference: first-message loss in local SB is purely a question of whether subscribe finishes before the first transmit, and apps must handle it themselves.\n(6) cFS 'launch-file' equivalents are tables and commands: SCH_LAB schedule, TO_LAB subscription table plus ground-commanded subscriptions, and the HK copy table with its own subscriptions. The MID a transmit sends is bound earlier through CFE_MSG_Init, and dispatch is a single pipe plus MsgId/FcnCode branches. So ROS-style recovery does not carry over directly.\n(7) HK already has a configurable freshness policy: DataPresent flags, a missing-data counter, and by default incomplete combined packets are still sent.\n(8) Timing analyses (Casini ECRTS'19 as implemented by its authors' crate; Becker/Günzel cause-effect chains) take chain structure plus WCET, periods and supply/demand models as inputs. They recover nothing from source.\n(9) Empirical ROS data: 19 of 221 ROBUST bugs carry CONCURRENCY codes, mostly thread-level. The note's formula RequiredHB AND NOT ProvenHB AND PotentialConcurrent is anticipated by TaxDC §8.4. TaxDC proposes detecting DC bugs by (1) obtaining order and atomicity timing specifications among messages and computation, and (2) detecting violations by static or dynamic analysis. Netzer & Miller's feasible vs apparent races (summary) back the revision's point that unproven HB is only a candidate.\n2. F1 first-message loss is a recognized hazard under other names:\n   - TaxDC \"message arrives unexpectedly early\" (m3274: an early kill message is incorrectly ignored) and the error symptom \"Global missing messages\".\n   - InitRacer's late-event-handler-registration (summary).\n   - P's \"unhandled event\" check with explicit deferred/ignored sets.\n   - ZeroMQ's \"slow joiner\" and ROS 2's transient-local durability for late joiners.\n3. F2 use-before-init is the classic order violation:\n   - Lu et al.: about one third of examined non-deadlock bugs.\n   - PCT Fig. 1(a): access before initialization is a depth-1 ordering bug.\n   - TaxDC h5780: a join request sent before a security-key message arrives makes initialization abort.\n   - Guard flags such as `attitude_valid` are ad hoc / value-dependent synchronization, already modeled by EventRacer (dynamic) and Schwarz et al. VMCAI 2014 (static; summary).\n4. F3 stale state has a direct static precedent: Burrows & Leino (full text). Their staleness is relative to critical-section boundaries, not physical age or the producer's publication count. Time-based freshness still needs R07-style data.\n5. F4 multi-variable consistency is well covered for shared memory: high-level data races / view consistency, atomicity types, AVIO, and TaxDC's atomicity violation. Inference: if cFS handlers run to completion on one app task, an epoch-mismatched pair (A_k, B_j) involves no interleaving inside a code block. Those definitions then do not apply directly. This category is the least covered by the checked definitions and needs an epoch/time-disparity specification.\n6. F5 startup dependency is covered as an order-violation class: Lu et al., TaxDC h5780 and its reboot-timing bugs, DroidRacer/SIERRA lifecycle models, Goblint's thread-creation MHP, DCatch's distributed while-loop sync rule, and Tulsyan's RTOS rules. What remains for cFS is domain modeling of ES startup semantics, not a new fault class.\n7. It can at most rest on cFS SB/ES/TBL-specific semantics, on message-derived state provenance, and on the F4 epoch property. The error 'arises when a lock is held at the time a value is read from a shared variable, but not held at the time the value is later used', and can occur even when every shared access is lock-protected. Detection: ghost booleans stale_t and from_critical_t per local variable, assert NOT stale_t before each read, and set stale_t on entry to a critical section if t's last write was inside a critical section. It is implemented as a compile-time checker in ESC/Java and needs no annotations. The checker mostly ignores which lock is acquired. Results: about 617 kloc, 43 false alarms, 1 benign race, 4 bugs. The authors note the technique can also be used dynamically.","evidence":["Full text (author manuscript): https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/12/krml107.pdf; local txt/burrows_leino_stale.txt lines 9-16, 48-54, 126-135, 201-213, 249-287, 363-395, 506-591, 676-690","Published: Concurrency and Computation: Practice and Experience 16(12), 2004, DOI 10.1002/cpe.866 (Wiley; not opened)"],"scope_checked":"Full text of MSR-hosted manuscript; not diffed against the Wiley published version","status":"confirmed","importance":"central","implication":"INFERENCE: The agenda's F3 'stale state' already has a precise static-analysis precedent; the note must cite it. Its staleness is relative to synchronization scopes, i.e. a snapshot crossing critical-section boundaries. It is not about physical age or about whether the producer has published a newer value. cFS state is copied from payloads into app globals, not locals. So the cFS notion needs a different abstraction (message epoch or receive event), and freshness in time still needs R07-type timing data (revision §6.5)."},{"id":"rw-races-06","topic":"High-level data races and block-local atomicity (Artho, Havelund, Biere)","claim":"Per search-engine summaries: high-level data races concern accesses to sets of related fields that should be accessed atomically. A 'view' of an atomic block is the set of shared variables it accesses, and maximal views are those not contained in another view. Two threads are view-consistent if the views of one, intersected with the maximal views of the other, form a chain under set inclusion. The authors argue the classical data-race notion misses such inconsistencies. The ATVA 2004 follow-up abstract says a data-flow-based static technique in JNuke finds stale-value errors that low- and high-level data race algorithms do not find, with no annotations.","evidence":["Bibliographic: STVR 13(4):207-227, 2003; author PDF URL https://people.kth.se/~artho/papers/stvr03.pdf (BLOCKED, 403)","ATVA 2004, LNCS 3299: https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-540-30476-0_16 (BLOCKED); author PDF https://people.kth.se/~artho/papers/bl-atomicity.pdf (BLOCKED)","WebSearch summaries obtained 2026-10-01 (queries on 'view consistency' and 'block-local atomicity')"],"scope_checked":"Search-engine summaries only; no full text; implementation (dynamic vs static) of the 2003 paper not verified","status":"unverified","importance":"central","implication":"INFERENCE: F4 'multi-stream inconsistent snapshot' must cite HLDR / view consistency as the established multi-variable consistency notion. In my probes on the LLVM dialect, the only upstream alias analysis said two distinct globals MayAlias and two different fields of one struct MustAlias. It loses the taint for double payloads, and across any opaque call such as the next ReceiveBuffer, even when the global is `static`. So handler-to-handler flow inside a cFS run loop is not reported without custom API modelling.\n(6) The alternatives were judged on four axes from their docs and source: (a) pub/sub graph recovery, (b) payload-to-state provenance, (c) cross-app ordering/HB, (d) source-located diagnostics. PhASAR, SVF, CodeQL, Joern, CSA, Frama-C, IKOS and Goblint can all do (b) and (d) to some degree. None provides (a) or (c) without user-written models. Goblint, SVF-MTA and RacerF reason about pthread/lock-based shared memory, not message-order happens-before.\n\nConclusion (inference): a custom dialect is justified only as a typed, verifiable container for the cFS API model (SB/ES/TBL operations with effects on non-addressable resources) and for task/app structure, plus reuse of MLIR's solver. Its costs are: writing the alias and memory model, closed-world call resolution and function-pointer handling, recovering MID and field names, and absorbing MLIR API churn. Effects go on non-addressable resources (pipe/MID queue, table buffer, ES state), so modelled calls stop clobbering all C memory. Upstream llvm.call is ModRef-all (rw-mlir-08) and main's LocalAliasAnalysis treats non-addressable effects as NoAlias.\n3. App/task/run-loop structure becomes regions, so inter-task reasoning is separate from intra-task dataflow.\n4. Reuse of the DataFlowSolver (sparse/dense, DCA, interprocedural walk).\n5. Location-carrying diagnostics: FileLineColLoc is preserved on calls and loads (rw-mlir-04).\n\nWhat it costs (all researcher-built):\n- a field-sensitive, global-aware memory model and alias analysis: upstream is field-insensitive and globals MayAlias (rw-mlir-08/09);\n- a closed-world visibility fix (rw-mlir-07) and indirect-call resolution (rw-mlir-10);\n- MID constant recovery and field-name and macro mapping (rw-mlir-05);\n- an SB model, which every tool needs anyway (rw-mlir-12);\n- a cross-task HB/interleaving layer, which MLIR does not provide (rw-mlir-06);\n- tracking an unstable MLIR analysis API (rw-mlir-18);\n- a frontend decision: the LLVM path works now; CIR is opt-in and untested here (rw-mlir-01/02).","evidence":["Synthesis of rw-mlir-04 through rw-mlir-18 (each with its own evidence)"],"scope_checked":"Synthesis; no implementation of the dialect was attempted beyond the IRDL structural toy (rw-mlir-11).","status":"partial","importance":"central","implication":"Inference for the research design:\n- The comparison a reviewer will ask for is 'same SB model in CodeQL (isAdditionalFlowStep) or PhASAR/SVF (IFDS + points-to)' versus 'cfs dialect + MLIR solver', on the same cFS apps.\n- MLIR's claimed benefit must be shown on (c), ordering and HB across apps with typed effects, and on the cost of extension, not on (a) or (b) alone.\n- Keep the LLVM-dialect frontend for the MVP with DI-based field mapping, and treat CIR or VAST as an ablation once a CIR-enabled build is pinned."}],"gaps":["ClangIR could not be run on any C code here. read the SB and OSAL queue code at pinned commits: cFE 546a0025 and OSAL dad0ee99. OSAL dad0ee99 is the osal submodule of the nasa/cFS default branch, dev, at 5a9b075c, and that branch also pins cFE 546a0025. The cFS main branch at 088b2fa8 pins a different cFE (c5fb2b4d) and OSAL d2d877a6. Its queue files are byte-identical to dad0ee99. I also built cFE with SIMULATION=native, using PSP 53df1d54, and ran my own probe app, sbprobe, in five configurations. The run logs are in probe_runs/.\n\nSource and probe runs agree on these points:\n- TransmitMsg copies the message into a shared-pool buffer. Under the SB mutex it bumps the per-route sequence counter and applies the MsgLim check while walking the destination list from the head, which is the last subscriber. It then releases the mutex and calls OS_QueuePut once per pipe without holding the lock.\n- With real priorities on one CPU, higher-priority receivers ran before TransmitMsg returned in 200/200 trials. They woke in reverse subscription order.\n- A relay task with higher priority than the sender produced a cross-pipe causality inversion in 500/500 trials: the observer pipe got the relay's output before the original message. With a lower-priority relay this happened 0/500 times. On 4 CPUs it happened 0/500 times, and the wake order of three receivers covered all 6 permutations. As non-root, where priorities are silently ignored, it happened 15/500 and 30/500 times.\n- TransmitMsg returns CFE_SUCCESS when MsgLim is exceeded or a pipe is full. Only HK counters and events, which are filtered after the first 4 or 16 by default, show the loss.\n- Routes are append-only. The no-subscribers counter and event fire only for a MID that has never been subscribed since boot. After a subscriber unsubscribes or is cleaned up, sends are dropped silently and the sequence counter keeps advancing.\n- On Linux, POSIX mq with constant priority 1 and a non-blocking put gives per-pipe FIFO (confirmed in the Linux v6.12 source and the mq_send man page). There is no global order across pipes.\n- The native sample config enables OSAL permissive mode. As non-root, a pipe depth of 20 was silently truncated to msg_max=10, so 10 messages overflowed.\n- The default origination action stamps telemetry with the time of transmit.\n- A MsgId is a struct around a uint32. Non-EDS MIDs are compile-time base|topic values. EDS MIDs come from runtime calls that depend on the PSP processor id.\n- An ES restart runs SB cleanup, which deletes the app's pipes and discards queued messages, before the tasks are deleted. The restarted app gets a new AppId.\n\nEach finding below labels the implication for static analysis as inference.","findings":[{"id":"sem-sb-01","topic":"TransmitMsg pipeline and locking","claim":"CFE_SB_TransmitMsg works in this order: (1) validate the MsgId and size from the header; (2) allocate a buffer from the shared SB pool and memcpy the whole message into it; (3) take the SB mutex to look up the route, increment the per-route sequence counter (only if the route exists and IsOrigination), walk the destination list from the head, apply MsgLim and do optimistic depth accounting, then release the mutex; (4) run CFE_MSG_OriginationAction; (5) call OS_QueuePut for each destination pipe without the SB mutex, taking the mutex again only to undo accounting on error; (6) drop the sender's reference. The same descriptor pointer is enqueued on every pipe, so fan-out is by reference.","evidence":["https://github.com/nasa/cFE/blob/546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f/modules/sb/fsw/src/cfe_sb_api.c#L1538-L1597","https://github.com/nasa/cFE/blob/546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f/modules/sb/fsw/src/cfe_sb_priv.c#L1032-L1131 (lock L1049, seq L1058-L1064, unlock L1114)","https://github.com/nasa/cFE/blob/546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f/modules/sb/fsw/src/cfe_sb_priv.c#L1173-L1231 (OS_QueuePut L1187-L1188 outside lock)","https://github.com/nasa/cFE/blob/546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f/modules/sb/fsw/src/cfe_sb_priv.c#L1265-L1297","https://github.com/nasa/osal/blob/dad0ee99c121f2ecc11f8c0798c1e836e41d8ab1/src/os/shared/src/osapi-queue.c#L190-L219 (OS_LOCK_MODE_NONE L200)","Stale comment: cfe_sb_api.c#L1564-L1575 says no buffer is allocated without a route, but the buffer is allocated at L1557 before routing"],"scope_checked":"Full text of cfe_sb_api.c (1636 lines), cfe_sb_priv.c (1778 lines), cfe_sb_buf.c, cfe_sb_priv.h structs, cfe_sbr_route_unsorted.c, cfe_sbr_map_hash.c, OSAL shared osapi-queue.c at the pinned commits","status":"confirmed","importance":"central","implication":"Inference: model one publish as several separate per-destination enqueue events that follow the call, not as one atomic broadcast. Linux-sim test results are not evidence for RTOS behaviour."},{"id":"sem-sb-03","topic":"No subscribers: counters, events, routes","claim":"A MID with no route (never subscribed since SB init) increments NoSubscribersCounter (uint8), raises a SEND_NO_SUBS INFORMATION event (EVS-filtered after the first 4 by default) and returns CFE_SUCCESS. Both cases are per-pipe failures: the message is dropped for that pipe only, counters and filtered ERROR events are raised, and TransmitMsg still returns CFE_SUCCESS. The analysis should carry MsgLim and depth as model parameters per (MID, pipe)."},{"id":"sem-sb-05","topic":"Per-pipe FIFO and queue platform semantics","claim":"On Linux, OSAL posix OS_QueuePut calls mq_timedsend with constant priority 1 and a zero absolute timeout, so it never blocks and returns OS_QUEUE_FULL when full. The flags argument is ignored; SB passes its timeout there. Linux mqueue appends same-priority messages at the tail and dequeues from the head, which gives per-pipe FIFO. The RTEMS impl uses rtems_message_queue_send, and RTEMS docs say it places the message at the rear. The VxWorks impl uses msgQSend(NO_WAIT, MSG_PRI_NORMAL) on a MSG_Q_FIFO queue; I did not check vendor docs for that. Each pipe has a single reader by design, because the previous buffer is released when the next receive starts.","evidence":["https://github.com/nasa/osal/blob/dad0ee99c121f2ecc11f8c0798c1e836e41d8ab1/src/os/posix/src/os-impl-queues.c#L285-L323","https://github.com/nasa/osal/blob/dad0ee99c121f2ecc11f8c0798c1e836e41d8ab1/src/os/inc/osapi-queue.h#L117-L133 (flags reserved)","https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.12/ipc/mqueue.c#L191-L231 and #L248-L288 (list_add_tail / list_first_entry)","https://github.com/mkerrisk/man-pages/blob/ae6b221882ce71ba82fcdbe02419a225111502f0/man3/mq_send.3#L71-L76","https://github.com/nasa/osal/blob/dad0ee99c121f2ecc11f8c0798c1e836e41d8ab1/src/os/rtems/src/os-impl-queues.c#L98-L103 and #L253-L256; RTEMS/rtems-docs b1afc8fb c-user/message/directives.md L589, operations.md L57-L58","https://github.com/nasa/osal/blob/dad0ee99c121f2ecc11f8c0798c1e836e41d8ab1/src/os/vxworks/src/os-impl-queues.c#L76 and #L186","https://github.com/nasa/cFE/blob/546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f/modules/sb/fsw/src/cfe_sb_priv.c#L1372-L1389"],"scope_checked":"OSAL posix/shared queue files in full; RTEMS and VxWorks put/create functions; Linux v6.12 mqueue.c insert/get/send sections; man-pages mq_send.3 text. traced cFE ES startup and lifecycle, Table Services, EVS registration and TIME startup in the source at cFE 546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f. For the runs I picked OSAL 5befd8e9 and PSP 36c24cb9 by date: the last dev merges before the cFE commit. The blob SHAs of the five files the revision note cites (cfe_es.h, cfe_sb.h, cfe_tbl.h, cfe_es_api.c, cfe_sb_priv.c) match its S08–S12 records. I then built the pinned core natively (with elf2cfetbl f97ba870) and ran five small probe apps of my own. They tested startup order against priority, the sync APIs, Table Services semantics and app restart, under four scheduling setups: RT privileges or not, all 4 CPUs or pinned to one. All runs used uid 65534, because root in this container cannot create SB queues deeper than msg_max=10.\n\nMain results:\n(1) Core apps now start strictly one after another, ES→EVS→SB→TBL→TIME. All EarlyInit runs before any task exists, and each core app must reach RUNNING before the next is created. Task priority does not change this order. The cFE #73 core-startup race therefore cannot be reproduced at this commit.\n(2) OPERATIONAL is reached after two 1000 ms timeouts even when apps are not RUNNING, and WaitForSystemState still returns success. Calling it also marks the caller's own app state ready. WaitForStartupSync discards the status, and its header promise to round the timeout up to 1000 ms is not implemented.\n(3) On Linux, OSAL picks SCHED_RR; without privileges it falls back silently in permissive mode. Priority-ordered app startup appeared only with RT privileges and a single pinned CPU (5/5 runs). Otherwise the startup-script order appeared, and not always.\n(4) CFE_TBL_GetAddress on a table that was never loaded returns a NULL pointer, both in the code and at runtime. The header promises a valid zero-filled pointer, and the revision note §7.1 repeats that promise as confirmed fact, so it needs correcting.\n(5) Table locking is one flag per handle, not per pointer. A second GetAddress moves the protection to the new buffer, and the first pointer's contents then changed under the sharer.\n(6) A single-buffered update is deferred (CFE_TBL_INFO_TABLE_LOCKED) until the address is released and Manage is called again. A double-buffered update goes ahead while a sharer holds the old buffer, and the next load is refused (CFE_TBL_ERR_NO_BUFFER_AVAIL).\n(7) When a table owner is restarted while another app shares its table, the new instance's Register fails with CFE_TBL_ERR_DUPLICATE_NOT_OWNED until the sharer calls Unregister. Meanwhile the sharer gets CFE_TBL_ERR_UNREGISTERED. This is reproduced, and gives source and runtime support to the original note's \"cross-application stale handle\" idea.\n(8) Restart, reload and delete only record a request; ES does the work later from a background scan. Teardown releases EVS, SB (subscriptions and queued messages dropped), TBL and TIME resources before it deletes the app's tasks. The restarted app gets a new AppId; restart took about 1.6 s in these runs.\n\nOne concurrency candidate is from code reading only: the owner's CFE_TBL_Load releases the registry lock before it applies the update. All findings separate confirmed source, confirmed runtime and inference. WebFetch on github.com HTML worked, but it shows only issue bodies and metadata, and it paraphrases rather than quoting verbatim. Comments, including the migrated Trac comments, were not visible. Blobless git clones (11 MB) gave me the full commit history and source at any revision.\n\n**Volume examined:**\n- 37 issue-search pages, about 379 listing rows including duplicates.\n- 42 individual issue/PR pages.\n- 8,198 commit subjects, filtered by keyword to 478, of which 25 key commits were read in full.\n- Source files checked at several revisions: cFE 6.5.0a (commit 792f5e35), 6.6.0a (2661d19f), v6.7.0, v7.0.0, the pinned commit 546a002, sample_app dev 199476a and MD HEAD 65eb7b3.\n\n**Result:** 38 candidates recorded in candidates.json. About 26 fall in the agenda categories; the rest are lifecycle/shutdown items or excluded shared-memory races.\n\n**cFE #73 and #71:** I verified the bodies. Both were bulk-created on GitHub on 2019-09-30 with milestone 6.4.2. The original Trac dates could not be re-checked because comments were not readable. Neither has a fix commit on GitHub. The earliest public cFE source (6.5.0a) already contains the mitigations:\n- a wait after each core app starts;\n- an EVS_AppID range guard whose comment names the \"called before CFE_EVS_TaskInit\" case;\n- semaphore-free polling of the startup state.\n\nSo the original #73/#71 failures cannot be reproduced from public source.\n\n**Best reproducible cases** (code from before and after the fix is public, or the fault is still present):\n- cFE #198 (cross-app late-init race; 6.5.0a vs 6.6.0a).\n- CF #184 (cross-app startup race on a semaphore owned by another app; in-code comment states the race).\n- sample_app #101 (table address never released on the INFO_UPDATED path; introduced by the #28 fix).\n- HS #148 and MD #79 (table-derived state used before it is loaded; MD #79 confirmed in code at HEAD).\n- cFE #46 (multi-field TIME reference read; pre-fix, post-fix and current code all public).\n- cFE #950 (exit/cleanup race; recipe in the issue).\n- to_lab d3d52da (intentional late subscription, usable as a benign case).\n\n**Key finding on table semantics:** At commit 546a002 the cfe_tbl.h comment for CFE_TBL_ERR_NEVER_LOADED (lines 540-544) no longer matches the code. The header says a valid zero-content pointer is returned and must be released. The implementation (cfe_tbl_registry.c lines 192-196) returns NULL and sets no lock. The venue correction also matters for citation accuracy.","votes":[{"refuted":false,"corrected_claim":"ROSDiscover (Timperley, Dürschmid, Schmerl, Garlan, Le Goues) appeared at ICSA 2022, not ICSE 2022: IEEE, pp. 112-123, DOI 10.1109/ICSA53651.2022.00019, per ROSInfer's reference list. The rosdiscover-evaluation README says \"ICSA, 2021\", which is most likely a year typo. ROSDiscover works from ROS 1 C++ source. It uses Clang ASTs, reachability analysis and symbolic function summaries to build component models: topic publish/subscribe ports, service ports (calls and provided services) and parameters. The paper says it focuses on \"topic, service, and action ports\", and parameters are modelled but not called ports. Launch files supply the system composition (nodes, parameters, remappings). Bug detection checks four first-order-logic well-formedness rules in Acme: one message-type mismatch rule and three variants of dangling publishers or subscribers using similar names or types. No ordering, startup or timing property is checked. The paper notes that domain experts could add more rules but did not explore this.\n\nROSInfer (ICSE 2024, DOI 10.1145/3597503.3639206) extends ROSDiscover. It infers component state machines statically from ROS C++ source. Input triggers are subscribed messages, periodic triggers, and component events such as \"component started\". State variables are found with two heuristics. The usage heuristic: the variable appears in the control conditions of architecturally relevant behaviour (functions that send messages, functions that change state variables, and their transitive callers). The scope heuristic: the variable is global or component-wide. State changes are inferred from assignments to these variables, with triggers inferred the same way. The paper does not require the assignment to be in a subscriber callback. The code at rosdiscover@78301b8, states_analyzer.py L30-42, requires an assignment in a function other than the publishing one; L53-68 then splits assignments into subscriber-callback and main-entry ones. ROSInfer emits PlusCal/TLA+ and checks user LTL properties or auto-generated \"expected output eventually happens\" properties. With a given component list, desired output and parameter assignments, it detected three known bugs from the ROSDiscover dataset that ROSDiscover could not find (autoware-02, -03, -10). In each, a required input is not connected to any publisher, so the component waits indefinitely. It does not model message contents (\"we do not model the content of messages\"). In future work the authors note that static analysis cannot infer execution times, so its models \"cannot be used for most kinds of performance analysis, bottleneck analysis, or analysis of race condition\". Both papers name NASA F Prime (F′) as a framework the approach could generalise to.","evidence":["Re-downloaded both PDFs myself. They were three known dataset bugs, re-detected given a component list, a desired output and parameter values.\n\n7. The quotes about message content not being modelled, the race-condition limitation (with its \"most kinds of\" hedge), the TLA+/LTL pipeline, and F Prime as a generalisation target all match the full text, and all cited line numbers check out.\n\nFor the note's novelty framing: neither paper mentions cFS. The thesis proposal planned dynamic timing extensions, and I could not check the 2025 dissertation. The note should therefore bound novelty against ROSInfer as published, and state that later work by the same author was not checked."},{"refuted":false,"corrected_claim":"ROSDiscover was published at ICSA 2022 (19th IEEE ICSA, pp. 112-123, DOI 10.1109/ICSA53651.2022.00019), not ICSE 2022. It targets ROS 1 C++ code. It builds component models from Clang ASTs plus symbolic function summaries, and launch files supply the composition. The paper says it focuses on topic, service and action ports. It also recovers component parameters, which the Acme style models as parameter-service ports. The paper picks four topic rules: message-type mismatch and three dangling publisher/subscriber heuristics. The ROSFam.acme it ships also has other structural rules: no service or action call to self, parameter reader and writer existence, no duplicate nodes, one bus per topic, and nodelet grouping. None of these rules, in the paper or in ROSFam.acme, states an ordering, startup or timing property.\n\nROSInfer (ICSE 2024, DOI 10.1145/3597503.3639206) is an extension of ROSDiscover. It infers component state machines from ROS C++ code using ROS 1 APIs. Transitions are driven by subscriber callbacks, and the paper also lists service callbacks (advertiseService) and message_filters registerCallback. Periodic triggers (Rate/Timer/sleep loops) and component events such as \"component started\" also drive transitions. The paper's state-variable heuristics are:\n- the variable is global or component-scoped;\n- it is used in control conditions of architecturally relevant behaviour (message-sending code, state-changing code and their transitive callers).\n\nIn the rosdiscover code at commit 78301b86, a state variable is one that appears in a publish call's inter-procedural condition and is assigned in some function other than the publishing one. Assignment does not have to happen in a subscriber callback; assignments are then split into subscriber-callback and main/entry-point groups. ROSInfer generates PlusCal/TLA+. Users either give LTL properties or a list of expected outputs, from which it auto-generates LTL properties. Applied to three already-known Autoware bugs from the ROSDiscover dataset (autoware-02, -03, -10), with a given component list, desired output and parameter values, it detected all three. In each, a required input has no publisher. Its formalism states 'we do not model the content of messages'. In future work the authors say static analysis cannot infer execution times, 'producing models that cannot be used for most kinds of performance analysis, bottleneck analysis, or analysis of race condition'. Both papers name NASA F Prime as a framework the approach could generalize to.","evidence":["Venue, ROSDiscover: author bib files found by GitHub code search all give ICSA '22, pp. 112-123, doi 10.1109/ICSA53651.2022.00019. Table 2 counts 46 bugs (44%) caused only by order violations, 21 (20%) only by atomicity violations, and 4 (4%) by multiple timing conditions. Finding #1 says 64% of the bugs are triggered by untimely messages, 32% by untimely faults or reboots, and 4% by both. Table 1 lists 'Global missing messages' and 'Global unexpected messages' among the error symptoms (§4). §8.4 ('Bug Detection Tools', part of §8 'Lessons Learned') proposes a generic detection framework with two tasks: (1) obtain timing specifications, i.e. order and atomicity specifications among messages and computation; (2) detect violations of them through dynamic or static analysis. It offers invariant templates such as 'message bc should arrive at C before message ac (ca) arrives (leaves)'. I downloaded the cited PDF myself, confirmed the hash, re-extracted the text, and checked each sub-claim against it and against the authors' ASPLOS'16 slides. Every factual element holds: the 104 bugs and four systems, the order-violation definition, the 44% and 64% figures, the two Table 1 symptom names, the §8.4 two-step framework with 'dynamic or static analysis' and the invariant template, and the m3274 and h5780 descriptions. I found no other version or later correction that contradicts any of them, but my search was narrow: web search was exhausted and ACM, Crossref and the uchicago sites were blocked. So refuted=false. The claim is imprecise in five places, fixed in corrected_claim. (a) 44% means bugs caused ONLY by order violations; 4% more have mixed timing. (b) 'Global unexpected messages' in Table 1 is called 'wrong messages' in §4.1/Table 3. 'Global missing messages' means the triggering node never sends an expected reply, not that a message is lost; mapping F1 (publish before subscriber ready) to 'Global missing messages' would misuse the term. (c) §8.4 is a research direction, not an implemented or evaluated detector. It also covers error-guided spec inference, which is directly relevant to the note's open 'how to obtain RequiredHB' question (§48). (d) The template quote is truncated; the full text is 'message bc should arrive at C before message ac (ca) arrives (leaves)'. (e) The DOI appears only in the authors' PDF; the publisher site was blocked. On why_central, which is inference and not part of the factual claim: TaxDC does show that 'required order spec + static/dynamic violation detection' was proposed before for message-timing bugs, so the original note must cite it. But TaxDC's model is multi-node and shared-nothing; it calls intra-node thread-interleaving bugs LC. cFS apps are tasks on one node exchanging Software Bus messages, so calling F1 and F5 instances of TaxDC's class is an analogy, not something TaxDC states. Its system model is a set of shared-nothing nodes. I checked the full text of the authors' self-hosted PDF, not the ACM DL copy. §3.1 defines a message-timing order violation as a bug that manifests whenever a message comes earlier (or later) than another event, either another message or a local computation, but not when it comes in the other order. 46 bugs (44%) are caused only by order violations. Finding #1 says 64% of the bugs are triggered by untimely messages (44% order plus 20% atomicity violations), and another 4% by a combination of messages and faults. Table 1 lists 'Global missing messages' and 'Global unexpected messages' among the error symptoms. §8.4 is in the 'Lessons Learned' discussion. It neither implements nor evaluates a detector. It states that message-timing DC bug detection 'should focus on' two tasks: (1) obtaining timing specifications, meaning order and atomicity specifications among messages and computation, and (2) detecting violations of them through dynamic or static analysis. A separate 'Invariant-guided detection' paragraph suggests templates for learned likely invariants, such as 'message bc should arrive at C before message ac (ca) arrives (leaves)'. The paper gives two examples. In m3274, a task-kill message from the AppMaster reaches the NodeManager before the task-init message and is incorrectly ignored. The paper explicitly calls using a snapshot after release 'justifiable' and does not flag it. Warnings come only after a later critical-section entry, such as re-acquisition after wait(), and in that case the lock IS held at use time. For the note's F3 framing, the boundary that matters is the re-entry or re-acquisition boundary, not simply 'after release'.\n(2) The claim says staleness is not tied to whether the producer published a newer value. That is right for the detector, which never tracks actual writes. But the paper's concept is motivated by possible intervening writes by other threads, so the checker should be described as a conservative re-entry proxy for 'possibly superseded', not as unrelated to newer writes.\n(3) The results omit two caveats: the authors say they are unsure of the categorization for ambit and jigsaw (the benign race and 22 false alarms come from those two programs), and the implementation covers only locals.\n(4) The bibliographic details are stated as checked fact, but the manuscript carries no venue information. Only processes, blackboards, semaphores, events, timed waits and the error handler become model actions for the graph and Promela output. ARINC's message-passing IPC, the closest analogue to the cFS Software Bus, is therefore not extracted. This narrows the why_central framing: the prior art recovered process, synchronisation and blackboard semantics from C into Promela or dot, but not queuing/sampling message flow. It is still relevant prior art for 'recovering RTOS API semantics from C', but not for message-ordering extraction. Goblint recovered RTOS process lifecycle and intra-partition synchronisation (semaphores, events, blackboard operations as events, and timeouts) from C by recognising API calls, and exported graphviz/Promela models. It did not model inter-partition message-port dataflow, which is the closest analogue to the cFS Software Bus. The prior-art point still holds as a general method: recognising RTOS API calls in C and exporting a process/synchronisation model. But it should not be described as message-IPC semantics recovery.\n\nThere are also smaller precision problems. a8a2e9c4 is the annotated tag object, not the commit; the commit is 5e1a53e2. (a) A transmit on a MID that has never been subscribed since boot increments the uint8 NoSubscribersCounter, raises CFE_SB_SEND_NO_SUBS_EID (EVS-filtered after the first 4 by default) and returns CFE_SUCCESS. (b) Routes are never removed. After an Unsubscribe or a pipe deletion, sends with zero destinations produce no counter and no event, return CFE_SUCCESS, and still advance the per-route sequence counter. (c) Exceeding MsgLim (default 4 per MID and pipe) or a full pipe drops the message for that pipe only, raises counters and filtered events, and TransmitMsg still returns CFE_SUCCESS; cFE's own functional test sb_sendrecv_test.c asserts this. (d) CFE_SB_Qos_t is documented as 'currently unused'. Quality is used only for subscription reports, and SB has no durability, latching or last-value retention for late subscribers. If publish can fail silently and there is no framework latching, the analyzer must reason about subscribe-versus-publish order itself.","votes":[{"refuted":false,"corrected_claim":"At cFE 546a0025, a failed local SB delivery never shows up in the publisher's return code. Each part holds with these qualifications.\n\n(a) A transmit on a MID that has no SB route returns CFE_SUCCESS. A route exists only after some Subscribe, SubscribeEx or SubscribeLocal call has created it since SB init. The send increments the uint8 HK NoSubscribersCounter and requests CFE_SB_SEND_NO_SUBS_EID (INFORMATION). The event is actually emitted only if two conditions hold:\n- SB's single FIRST_4_STOP filter for EID 14 is not yet used up. This filter belongs to the SB app and is shared by every MID and sender since boot. In the native sample boot, core-app startup uses 3 of the 4 before any app runs.\n- The publishing app is registered with EVS and has INFORMATION events enabled. EVS_IsFiltered calls CFE_EVS_GetTypeEnable, which looks up the calling task's AppId, not SB's.\n\n(b) Routes are never removed. Unsubscribe and DeletePipe only unlink destination nodes. When a route exists but has zero destinations, a send produces no counter and no event and returns CFE_SUCCESS. When IsOrigination=true it also still advances the per-route sequence counter.\n\n(c) A message is dropped for that pipe only in two cases:\n- MsgLim is reached (BuffCount >= MsgLim). MsgLim caps how many messages of that MID can sit unread in that pipe; CFE_SB_Subscribe sets it to 4 by default.\n- The OS queue is full (OS_QUEUE_FULL).\nIn either case MsgLimitErrorCounter or PipeOverflowErrorCounter increments, MsgSendErrorCounter also increments, and an ERROR event is requested (FIRST_16_STOP filter, gated by the same publisher event-type check). TransmitMsg still returns CFE_SUCCESS. sb_sendrecv_test.c asserts CFE_SUCCESS for the MsgLim case (L103-L107 with L166-L168). It asserts the same for MsgLim and pipe-depth drops in TestMsgBroadcast (L242-L250, L300-L304).\n\n(d) CFE_SB_Qos_t is documented as currently unused and intended for interprocessor communication. Quality is only copied into the subscription report, and only when reporting is enabled. SB has no durability, latching or last-value retention: a route entry holds only the destination list, MsgId and SeqCnt, and a new subscription starts empty.\n\nProbe runs 1-5:\n- T1: NoSub delta 1 for a never-subscribed MID versus 0 for a subscribed-then-unsubscribed one.\n- T2: sequence count went 1 -> 5 across 3 sends with zero destinations.\n- T3: MsgLim=2 with 5 sends gave 2 received, MsgLimitErrorCounter +3 and MsgSendErrorCounter +3, with all return codes 0.\n\nNone of these runs logged any NO_SUBS, Msg Limit or Pipe Overflow event from the probe, because the probe never called CFE_EVS_Register.","evidence":["Local clones sem-sb/cFE and probe/cFS/cfe are both at 546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f with clean working trees. Each one says what the claim attributes to it:\n- No route: the counter increments, the NO_SUBS event is requested, and the status stays CFE_SUCCESS.\n- Routes are never removed.\n- With zero destinations a send produces no counter and no event, and the sequence counter still advances.\n- MsgLim and pipe-full drops are per pipe; they increment counters and the send still returns CFE_SUCCESS.\n- The QoS typedef is documented as unused, and Quality only reaches the subscription report.\n- A route entry has no message storage.\n- The probe numbers are quoted exactly from the logs.\n\nSo the core point holds: the publisher's return code never reveals a failed delivery, and the analyzer must reason about subscribe-versus-publish order itself. I do not refute the claim.\n\nIt is imprecise in five places:\n1. It says the events are \"raised\" as if unconditionally. EVS_IsFiltered gates every event on CFE_EVS_GetTypeEnable, which reads the calling (publisher) task's AppId. A publisher that is not EVS-registered, or has INFORMATION/ERROR disabled, gets no NO_SUBS, MsgLim or Q_FULL event. This is why none of the five cited probe runs logged any such event: the probe never registered with EVS. I confirmed the mechanism by re-running with CFE_EVS_Register, and the events then appeared.\n2. \"Filtered after the first 4\" is one SB-app-wide filter shared by every MID and sender since boot, not a per-MID budget. In the native sample boot, 3 of the 4 are used by core startup, and my re-run showed a second never-subscribed MID producing no event.\n3. SB increments the uint8 NoSubscribersCounter and raises CFE_SB_SEND_NO_SUBS_EID. For sends with IsOrigination=true it still advances the per-route sequence counter.\n\n(c) If a destination is at its MsgLim, the message is dropped for that pipe only. The default for CFE_SB_Subscribe is CFE_PLATFORM_SB_DEFAULT_MSG_LIMIT = 4; SubscribeEx and SubscribeLocal take an explicit limit. A full queue or a queue write error also drops the message for that pipe only, because OS_QueuePut never blocks at this OSAL: its flags are \"reserved/unused\", and the POSIX, RTEMS and VxWorks backends send without waiting. TransmitBufferWithRoute's TimeOut therefore has no effect. In each case SB increments MsgLimitErrorCounter, or PipeOverflowErrorCounter or InternalErrorCounter. Under the SB mutex it looks up the route. When the route exists and IsOrigination is true, it also increments the per-route sequence counter, even if the route has no destinations left. It then walks the destination list from its head. Because Subscribe inserts new destinations at the head, cfe_sb.h and cfe_sb.dox say 'the message will first be sent to the last subscriber'. Under the lock it skips inactive destinations and IGNOREMINE self-pipes. It also drops any destination that is at its MsgLim; that drop only bumps a counter and produces no OS_QueuePut. The walk stops at CFE_PLATFORM_SB_MAX_DEST_PER_PKT entries. After unlocking, it runs CFE_MSG_OriginationAction (timestamp and checksum). It then calls OS_QueuePut without the mutex, once for each recorded pipe that did not hit MsgLim, and takes the mutex again only to undo the accounting when a put fails. cfe_sb.h says a higher-priority task that is pending and subscribed may run before TransmitMsg returns. On Linux the OSAL POSIX put calls mq_timedsend with constant priority 1 and an already-expired absolute timeout ({0,0}) on a blocking queue. Same-priority Linux mqueues are FIFO (checked in v6.18), so each pipe is FIFO in put order. A full queue returns ETIMEDOUT, which becomes OS_QUEUE_FULL: the message is dropped and only PipeOverflowErrorCounter changes, while TransmitMsg still returns CFE_SUCCESS. There is no order across pipes. Native-sim measurements (cFE 546a0025, OSAL dad0ee99, PSP 53df1d54, permissive mode compiled in):\n- One CPU, SCHED_RR active (runs 1 and 5, 200 trials each): in every trial all 3 receivers, which ran at higher priority than the sender, ran before TransmitMsg returned, in reverse subscription order.\n- Relay inversion: the relay's pipe was subscribed after the observer's, so it sat at the list head. In that design, a relay woken by the first put cannot transmit until the sender has finished every put. I infer that a broadcast was then effectively atomic with respect to other SB transmits and that the measured inversion would not occur, but I did not run this. This does not refute a claim pinned to 546a0025. Only if the route is valid AND IsOrigination is true does it increment the per-route sequence counter and write it into the copy (L1055-L1064). It walks the destination list from its head (L1067-L1089) and records each destination that is active and is not the sender's own IGNOREMINE pipe, up to MaxPipes (16 by default). Subscribe inserts at the head (CFE_SB_AddDestNode, priv L361-L391), and cfe_sb.h L238-L244 says 'the message will first be sent to the last subscriber'. The mutex is released at L1114. The origination action (timestamp, checksum) then runs unlocked. After that, OS_QueuePut is called once per recorded destination that is not marked, in head-first order, without the SB mutex (L1173-L1188, L1239-L1257); the mutex is re-taken only on error. cfe_sb.h L420-L422 says a pending higher-priority subscribed task 'may get to run before returning control to the caller'. In the OSAL POSIX implementation used on Linux (OSAL dad0ee99), OS_QueuePut_Impl calls mq_timedsend with constant priority 1 and a zeroed absolute timeout, ignoring the flags/timeout argument SB passes, and maps ETIMEDOUT to OS_QUEUE_FULL (os-impl-queues.c L285-L323). With Linux mqueue same-priority tail insertion, this gives per-pipe FIFO in the order of put calls and drop-on-full. It gives no global order across pipes; SB's MsgLim is a second, separate drop point. Measured on the native sim (cFE 546a0025, OSAL dad0ee99, PSP 53df1d54; one Linux 6.18 container, root, SCHED_RR active, all tasks observed on CPU 0; the pinning command is not recorded in the logs): in each of two runs (run1, run5), 3 receivers at higher OSAL priority (50, versus sender 100) all ran before TransmitMsg returned in 200/200 trials and woke in reverse subscription order in 200/200. In the specific setup where the relay's pipe was subscribed AFTER the observer's pipe (so it is at the list head and written first), a relay that outranked the sender made the observer dequeue the relay's output before the trigger in 500/500 trials in each of those two runs, versus 0/500 when the relay ranked below the sender. Other subscription orders were not tested. On 4 CPUs (runs 2 and 3, root) there were 0/500 inversions in both relay configurations; this is an observed timing outcome, not an ordering guarantee. Only 6/200 and 3/200 trials had all receivers run before return, and wake order took all 6 permutations in each run. One non-root run, also on CPU 0 with msg_max=10, used SCHED_OTHER because OSAL permissive mode silently falls back when it cannot set priorities (os-impl-tasks.c L320-L446). It gave 15/500 inversions in the 'relay higher' configuration and 30/500 in the 'relay lower' configuration; those priority labels have no effect under SCHED_OTHER.","evidence":["Local clone sem-sb/cFE at HEAD 546a002515be5a1e3b66f9ae2c14f948d9cec76f (clean); blob cfe_sb_priv.c 1de11a19, cfe_sb.h cf1413c1. It increments only when IsOrigination is true and the route is valid, which matters for relays and forwarders that pass false.\n(2) 'OS_QueuePut once per destination pipe' leaves out the Active, IGNOREMINE, MaxPipes=16 and MsgLim filters. Reaching OPERATIONAL does not mean every startup-script app is RUNNING. The core apps are different: CFE_ES_CreateObjects waits up to CFE_PLATFORM_CORE_MAX_STARTUP_MSEC (default 30000 ms) for each core app to reach RUNNING and calls CFE_PSP_Panic if it does not (cfe_es_start.c L859-872).\n\n(a) CFE_ES_Main moves SystemState from EARLY_INIT through CORE_STARTUP, CORE_READY, APPS_INIT and OPERATIONAL. It runs two separate waits, one for every in-use app record to reach LATE_INIT and one for RUNNING. Each wait is bounded by CFE_PLATFORM_ES_STARTUP_SCRIPT_TIMEOUT_MSEC, a mission-overridable platform parameter documented as a soft limit, with a default of 1000 ms (cfe_es_internal_cfg.h L862-879; sample_defs example_platform_cfg.h L856). On timeout ES only writes a syslog line and continues (cfe_es_start.c L204-229).\n\n(b) CFE_ES_WaitForSystemState runs under the ES lock first. If the caller has an app record, it raises the caller's own AppState when that state is lower. For non-core apps the mapping is OPERATIONAL→RUNNING, APPS_INIT→LATE_INIT and SHUTDOWN→STOPPED. For core apps, any state at or above CORE_READY maps to RUNNING. It then polls SystemState without the lock and returns CFE_ES_OPERATION_TIMED_OUT if the timeout expires. A successful return only means SystemState reached the requested state, which ES may have entered after its own sync timed out. There is no infinite-wait option. CFE_ES_RunLoop is the only other routine that raises AppState to RUNNING (cfe_es_api.c L469-472).\n\n(c) CFE_ES_WaitForStartupSync is a void wrapper that discards the status (L616-619). Two independent things close it. CFE_ES_CreateObjects runs EarlyInit for every core-object-table entry (CFE_CONFIG, ES, EVS, FS, SB, TBL, TIME, plus MSG/SBR/RESOURCEID entries that have no hooks) before any core-app task is created. PSP/OSAL threads such as the soft timebase already exist at that point. It then creates the main tasks of the entries that have a TaskMain (ES, EVS, SB, TBL, TIME; FS has none), one at a time, in the default MISSION_CORE_MODULES order, which a mission can change. After each one it calls CFE_ES_MainTaskSyncDelay(RUNNING, CFE_PLATFORM_CORE_MAX_STARTUP_MSEC; default 30000) at cfe_es_start.c:861. That call waits until every used app record is RUNNING and panics on timeout. A core task sets itself to RUNNING inside CFE_ES_WaitForSystemState after its TaskInit returns, whether or not TaskInit succeeded. So the TaskInit calls are serialized, but child tasks (ES_BG_TASK, the TIME tone/1Hz tasks) run at the same time as later initialization. Separately, CFE_EVS_SendEventWithAppID (cfe_evs.c:185-189) and EVS_SendEvent (cfe_evs_utils.c:617-623) range-check the AppId through EVS_GetAppDataByID before any table lookup. In 24 probe runs over 4 scheduling configurations (RR, where CFE_TIME has the highest rtprio of the core main tasks at 75, and no-cap; all CPUs and CPU0 only), ES, EVS, SB, TBL and TIME initialized in that order every time. 'Not reproducible' is an inference from the code plus these runs, not a targeted reproduction attempt. On every successful call, with no check of SB's own init state, CFE_SB_CreatePipe sends a debug event with CFE_SB_Global.AppId (cfe_sb_api.c L272-281) and discards the return value. Subscribe and the TransmitMsg no-subscriber path do the same. CFE_SB_Global.AppId stays 0 (memset in CFE_SB_EarlyInit) until SB's TaskInit sets it (cfe_sb_task.c:128). So during ES/EVS TaskInit, EVS_GetAppDataByID(0) returns NULL and the call returns CFE_EVS_APP_ILLEGAL_APP_ID, which nothing checks. This is open issue cFE #2663 (2025-08-06). A proposed fix, PR #2820, was closed unmerged on 2026-09-08 with the author's note that 'the fix is vacuous'. The behavior is no longer unreproduced: a gdb run of the pinned native build reproduced it 24 times during ES and EVS init (2 pipe-created, 4 subscription-received, 18 send-no-subscribers events). A run where queue creation failed showed that SB's CreatePipe error events are dropped the same way.","evidence":["Pinned code (git show 546a0025 in sem-es-tbl/cFE): cfe_es_start.c L777-799 Phase 1 EarlyInit loop over CoreObjectTable; L801-873 Phase 2 creates one task, then L861 CFE_ES_MainTaskSyncDelay(CFE_ES_AppState_RUNNING, CFE_PLATFORM_CORE_MAX_STARTUP_MSEC), L864-871 panics on failure. CFE_ES_CreateObjects (cfe_es_start.c L767-876), running in the ES startup thread, first calls EarlyInit for every core-module object-table entry that has one: CONFIG, ES, EVS, FS, SB, TBL, TIME. Only then does it create any core-app task. msg, sbr and resourceid have no EarlyInit, and OS/PSP threads already exist at this point. It then creates the core-app tasks one at a time: ES, EVS, SB, TBL, TIME. FS has no task. This order comes from the default MISSION_CORE_MODULES list, which missions may change. After each task is created, the startup thread polls until every used app record reaches RUNNING, within CFE_PLATFORM_CORE_MAX_STARTUP_MSEC (default 30000; L861). If that times out, it calls CFE_PSP_Panic (L864-871). A core app reaches RUNNING only when it calls CFE_ES_WaitForSystemState(CORE_READY) after its TaskInit. That wait then blocks it until ES sets CORE_READY. Child tasks created during TaskInit (ES_BG_TASK, the TIME tone and 1Hz tasks) run concurrently. The #73 crash sequence is therefore not reachable in this code under the default core-module configuration. This is an inference from reading the code, not a failed reproduction attempt. Each descriptor holds a single (LockFlag, BufferIndex). GetAddress sets LockFlag and records the current active buffer, and ReleaseAddress only clears LockFlag, so a second GetAddress without a release moves protection to the new buffer. At runtime a sharer's never-released first pointer to double-buffered table D read value 3 instead of 1 after the owner's Load(v3). Update behaviour depends on buffering. For a single-buffered table, a load while a descriptor holds the buffer returns CFE_TBL_INFO_TABLE_LOCKED (0x4c000018) and stays pending until the address is released and Manage is called again (then INFO_UPDATED 0x4c00000e). The analyzer's TBL typestate must be per handle and depend on the buffering option.","votes":[{"refuted":false,"corrected_claim":"At cFE 546a0025, table locking is per access descriptor, not per pointer. A descriptor is one per table handle, and it carries the AppId and HandleId. Each descriptor holds one LockFlag and one BufferIndex.\n\n- **GetAddress:** CFE_TBL_TxnGetTableAddress sets LockFlag=true and records the ID of the current active buffer. It does not check whether the descriptor is already locked.\n- **ReleaseAddress:** it changes only LockFlag (sets it false). BufferIndex is left as it was. The notification lookup it also does is read-only.\n- **Consequence:** a second GetAddress without a release moves the protection to the then-active buffer.\n\nAt runtime this happened in all 4 probe runs (R1-R4). A sharer took a pointer to double-buffered table D, which read 1. It then called GetAddress(D) a second time without releasing (the second pointer read 2). After the owner's Load(D,v3), the never-released first pointer read 3.\n\nUpdate behaviour depends on buffering.\n\n- **Single-buffered (CFE_TBL_OPT_DEFAULT):** Load stages the data in a shared load buffer. The activation step (UpdateInternal → GetInactiveBufferExclusive) checks every descriptor of the table for a lock on the table's single buffer. If one is locked, Load returns CFE_TBL_INFO_TABLE_LOCKED (0x4c000018), which is a positive informational code. The load is left in progress. After the address is released, GetStatus reports UPDATE_PENDING (0x4c000004). The owner's Manage, or Update, then applies the load and Manage returns INFO_UPDATED (0x4c00000e).\n  - This was observed with the owner's own descriptor holding the lock, in the probe and in the cFE functional test. The functional test's own comment says the Manage call \"shouldn't be necessary\".\n  - The case where a sharer holds the lock follows from the same descriptor scan. That is inference from the code and was not run.\n- **Double-buffered:** Load writes the inactive buffer and makes it active. A sharer that holds the previously active buffer keeps seeing the old content (val 1 at t+3s).\n  - **When the next load is refused:** only while the holder's descriptor still points at the now-inactive buffer. CFE_ES_RestartApp, ReloadApp and DeleteApp only set ControlReq; the ES background scan later runs ProcessControlRequest, which calls CFE_ES_CleanUpApp and then AppCreate, giving a new AppId. CleanUpApp invokes the core-module cleanup callbacks before deleting the app's tasks. SB cleanup deletes every owned pipe: it removes the pipe from all routes, discards queued messages and deletes the OS queue. TBL releases all the app's access descriptors, and EVS unregisters the app. Routes and their sequence counters persist. That runs CFE_ES_CleanUpApp and then, for restart or reload, CFE_ES_AppCreate, which gives a new AppId. CleanUpApp invokes every core module's AppCleanupCb before it deletes the app's tasks and pools. Those callbacks are SB, TBL, EVS, TIME and a placeholder for TA. SB deletes every pipe the app owns: it removes the pipe from all routes, drains queued buffers and deletes the OS queue. It also releases the app's zero-copy buffers. TBL cancels dump requests for tables the app owns and releases all of the app's access descriptors. It also marks the app's owned tables as unowned (OwnerAppId=UNDEFINED). All of these hold, so the claim is not refuted.\n\nSearching for counter-evidence turned up one substantive limit and several imprecisions.\n\n(1) The DUPLICATE_NOT_OWNED dependency depends on the version. Every cFE version before the v7.0.0 'cFS Draco' squash commit c1ab1b7 (2026-01-22) cleared the table name during app cleanup and Unregister. That includes v6.7.0, v6.8.0, v7.0.0-rc1, draco-rc5 and dev at fcbb179 from 2025-12-17. In those versions the restarted owner's Register would not hit the unowned 'shadow' record; this is an inference from source and was not run. The official TBL FAQ at the same commit still describes that older behavior, so the docs and the code disagree. For the research note this means the behavior must not be generalized to cFS or to deployed missions. A benchmark or analyzer rule built on it must pin cFE 7.0.x or later, and the doc/code mismatch is itself worth reporting.\n\n(2) The cleanup list is incomplete:\n- TIME and TA callbacks also run.\n- SB also releases zero-copy buffers.\n- TBL also cancels dump requests and marks owned tables unowned while keeping their records.\n\n(3) 'Until the sharer calls Unregister' should read: until all remaining access links are released. I read the source directly and did not depend on fetches from blocked sites."},{"refuted":false,"corrected_claim":"At cFE 546a0025 (source reading), restart is an asynchronous request followed by teardown. CFE_ES_RestartApp, CFE_ES_ReloadApp and CFE_ES_DeleteApp do not tear anything down themselves. They validate the AppId, and Restart and Reload also check that the app file exists (otherwise CFE_ES_FILE_IO_ERR). Reload also overwrites StartParams.FileName. Each then sets ControlReq.AppControlRequest. Later, the ES background scan moves a RUNNING app with a pending request to WAITING, with a kill timer of APP_KILL_TIMEOUT x APP_SCAN_RATE (5 x 1000 ms in the sample config). It runs CFE_ES_ProcessControlRequest once that timer expires, or at the next scan after the app has called CFE_ES_ExitApp, which sets it to STOPPED with the timer at 0. ProcessControlRequest always calls CFE_ES_CleanUpApp. It calls CFE_ES_AppCreate, which gives a new AppId, only for RESTART and RELOAD, not for DELETE. CleanUpApp runs the core-module AppCleanupCb callbacks before it deletes the app's tasks. SB deletes every pipe the app owns: it removes the pipe from all routes, drains and discards the queued messages and deletes the OS queue; it also releases the app's zero-copy buffers. TBL drops dump requests for tables the app owns and releases all of the app's access descriptors. It marks tables the app owned as un-owned (OwnerAppId = UNDEFINED). Such a registry record is freed only when no access descriptors remain. EVS frees the app's EVS registration, and TIME clears its synch callback. SBR has no route-removal function, so routes and their sequence counters persist; this part is source-only and was not exercised in a restart run. Consequence: if another app still holds a Share handle to a table the restarted app owned, the new instance's CFE_TBL_Register returns CFE_TBL_ERR_DUPLICATE_NOT_OWNED (0xcc00000d). This lasts until every remaining access descriptor is released, by Unregister or by the sharer's own cleanup. Once cleanup has run, the sharer's GetAddress returns CFE_TBL_ERR_UNREGISTERED (0xcc000009) with a NULL pointer. Between the RestartApp call and cleanup (about 1.5 s here), the sharer still got the old buffer with status 0x4c00000e or 0. The TBL/ES part was observed once in each of the 4 probe configurations. All four are scheduling variants on one native Linux build with OSAL permissive mode: SCHED_RR with CAP_SYS_NICE or default TS, all CPUs or CPU0. In each run there was one sharer, and the owner exited cooperatively from a 500 ms RunLoop poll. Time from the RestartApp call to the new instance's entry was 1.57-1.63 s, and the AppId went 1114121 -> 1114123. Every mechanism the claim states is correct at the source level: the request sets a flag only, cleanup happens later in the background scan, the core-module callbacks run before task deletion, SB deletes pipes and drains queues, TBL releases access descriptors, EVS frees the app's registration, and the error codes and values match. The runtime observation also holds in all four R1-R4 logs. So the claim is not false, but it overreaches in several narrower ways.\n\n(1) The sentence groups DeleteApp with \"CleanUpApp and then AppCreate, giving a new AppId\", but AppCreate runs only for RESTART and RELOAD. (2) \"Only set ControlReq\" leaves out that ReloadApp rewrites the file name and that Restart and Reload can fail with FILE_IO_ERR. (3) \"Until the sharer calls Unregister\" generalizes from a probe with one sharer. The source condition is that every remaining access descriptor is released, by Unregister or by the sharer's own cleanup. (4) The sharer's UNREGISTERED/NULL result begins only after cleanup. For about 1.5 s after RestartApp the sharer still got the old buffer. (5) \"Reproduced in all 4 configurations\" rests on one restart run per configuration. The four configurations are only scheduling variants of one native Linux build in OSAL permissive mode, and only the ES/TBL part was exercised at runtime. SB pipe teardown and route persistence on restart are source-only. (6) The 1.6 s is about 1.57-1.63 s, and it depends on the sample scan rate and kill timeout and on the probe's cooperative 500 ms exit. A non-cooperating app would wait out the roughly 5 s kill timer. (7) The cleanup list leaves out TIME clearing synch callbacks, SB releasing zero-copy buffers, and TBL dropping dump requests and marking the app's tables un-owned. The un-owned marking is the actual cause of DUPLICATE_NOT_OWNED. The cited evidence also omits cfe_es_api.c, where the three request functions are, and the TxnGetTableAddress lines. These are precision fixes, not a refutation, so refuted=false and the corrected wording narrows the claim."}],"verdict":"confirmed"},{"cid":"C16","claim":"In the cFS bundle apps, much of the run-time pub/sub graph is set by data, configuration and ground commands rather than by literal API arguments. This holds even with -fdebug-macro: the .ll then carries DIMacro records (e.g. SAMPLE_APP_CMD_MID), but the import drops them without a warning. At -O0, code-defined MIDs reach CFE_SB_Subscribe and CFE_MSG_Init as llvm.call @CFE_SB_ValueToMsgId(llvm.mlir.constant), then a store into a CFE_SB_MsgId_t alloca, then a load, so the call-site operand is not a constant. In sample_app, 6275 (0x1883, SAMPLE_APP_SEND_HK_MID, sample_app.c:158) and 6274 (0x1882, SAMPLE_APP_CMD_MID, sample_app.c:173) go to Subscribe, and 2179 (0x0883, HK_TLM) goes to MSG_Init. Running the same mlir-opt passes on the -O0 IR is not enough: the alloca goes away, but CFE_SB_ValueToMsgId (static inline in cfe_sb.h, compiled noinline optnone at -O0) stays an llvm.call. Compiling with -O1 -Xclang -disable-llvm-passes and then running mlir-opt --inline --sroa --mem2reg --canonicalize --cse makes these direct llvm.mlir.constant operands. Counts are per source call site, deduplicated by debug location; raw post-inline counts are higher because init functions are inlined and also kept out of line. The counted APIs are Subscribe/SubscribeEx/Unsubscribe and MSG_Init; dispatch-side MID comparisons are not counted. It is consistent with the evidence for the LLVM-dialect path."},{"refuted":false,"corrected_claim":"Interpretation, supported by the evidence below: upstream MLIR (mlir/) provides the dataflow solver but no memory model suited to cFS-style C code. Experiments with local mlir-opt at 1053047a (Feb 2026) on small synthetic hand-written and clang-imported LLVM-dialect inputs, not on cFS code, showed the following. (a) LocalAliasAnalysis is the only AliasAnalysis implementation in MLIR core and the only one mlir::AliasAnalysis registers by default. (Elsewhere in llvm-project, Flang's FIR-specific fir::AliasAnalysis is registered by Flang passes and does not apply to LLVM-dialect IR.) It returns MayAlias for two distinct globals reached via llvm.mlir.addressof. It returns MustAlias for two different fields of one struct when both GEPs share a base SSA value, because GEP implements ViewLikeOpInterface and is unwrapped to its base. This was reproduced on clang -O0-imported C. The relevant code is unchanged on main ccac700c. (b) Every llvm.call is ModRef for every location, because llvm.call does not implement MemoryEffectOpInterface (unchanged on main ccac700c). This holds whether the callee is external or defined, even for non-escaping allocas, and even when the call carries memory_effects = none. (c) The LLVM-IR importer never sets sym_visibility, so imported C functions, including static/internal ones (seen at -O0; at -O1 the static function was inlined away), are public to MLIR. DeadCodeAnalysis therefore treats them as having unknown predecessors. Manually adding sym_visibility = \"private\" restores \"(all) predecessors\". (d) In mlir::CallGraph (-test-print-callgraph), an indirect call through a 2-entry static const function-pointer table gets only an edge to <Unknown-Callee-Node>, at both -O0 and -O2. (e) The upstream test-only pass -test-last-modified, which keys state by SSA underlying value, returns <unknown> for both a hand-written and a clang-imported LLVM-dialect encoding of the original note's section 44 HandleAttitude->SetState->global.att->Guidance example. Even within one function, a store and a load through two different addressof ops of the same global gives <unknown>. Note that Guidance has no call path from HandleAttitude, so <unknown> there is also expected without a cross-handler/run-loop model. Its body describes a startup segfault found while debugging on the Microblaze processor used by the EVA team at GRC. The core tasks are created in cfe_es_objtab.c order EVS, SB, ES, TIME, TBL. Their default priorities are 61, 64, 68, 60 and 70, so TIME's TaskMain runs first. TIME calls CFE_SB_CreatePipe, which calls CFE_EVS_SendEventWithAppID with CFE_SB.AppId, which is still 0. That reaches EVS_NotRegistered, then EVS_SendEvent, then EVS_IsFiltered with CFE_EVS_GlobalData.EVS_AppId = 0xFFFFFFFF. EVS_IsFiltered has no range check, so it segfaults. The out-of-range index was therefore EVS's own sentinel, not SB's AppId. The issue lists three problems: core apps should be synchronized to RunLoop, EVS_IsFiltered should range-check, and SB and EVS initialize their AppID globals differently. Pre-fix and post-fix source are both public on SourceForge. The cFE 6.4.1 OSS release (2014-12-12) has neither a per-core-app sync nor an EVS_AppID guard. The 6.4.2 OSS release (2015-07-13, matching the milestone) adds CFE_ES_ApplicationSyncDelay with CFE_PSP_Panic after each core task is created, and a guard `EVS_AppID < CFE_ES_MAX_APPLICATIONS` in EVS_SendEvent, the caller, not inside EVS_IsFiltered. The b2765d9f and 792f5e35 git diff for these files is empty. b2765d9f cfe/fsw/cfe-core/src/es/cfe_es_start.c:934-952 and 1009-1042 hold the sync delay plus panic. cfe/fsw/cfe-core/src/evs/cfe_evs_utils.c:616-617 holds the guard, while EVS_IsFiltered at L238-239 still has no check. cfe_evs_task.c:173 has `EVS_AppID = CFE_EVS_UNDEF_APPID`, the same as 6.4.1, and CFE_EVS_UNDEF_APPID is 0xFFFFFFFF per cfe_evs_task.h:105. (2017-06-29).","Related later record: nasa/cFE issue #2663 (open, created 2025-08-06), 'Unregistered AppId error with CFE_EVS_SendEventWithAppID() during cFE task startup'. It reports that SB API calls made during ES/EVS init still pass CFE_SB's AppId to EVS before SB is registered and get an error code back. So the SB/EVS AppID-before-registration ordering was never made consistent; it now fails without crashing (from the GitHub search_issues body)."],"reasoning":"The issue metadata and the crash mechanism hold up. I confirmed them against the issue body (fetched verbatim through the GitHub search API) and the code path. In pre-fix 6.4.1, AppID 0 passes the SendEventWithAppID range check and reaches EVS_NotRegistered and then EVS_SendEvent. EVS_SendEvent then indexes AppData[0xFFFFFFFF] in EVS_IsFiltered without a check. So the out-of-range index really is EVS's sentinel, not SB's AppId. Small details also match: the objtab order and the 61/64/68/60/70 priorities. The issue presents those priorities as the default/example config, not necessarily the Microblaze build's. However, three load-bearing sub-claims are wrong, and primary sources show it. (1) 'No public pre-fix code exists' is false. NASA's own SourceForge releases include cFE 6.4.1 (Dec 2014), which contains the pre-fix code, and 6.4.2 (July 2015), which contains the fix. The 6.4.2 VDD names the Trac ticket (#42) and says the races 'were found in cFE build 6.4.1'. This directly changes how the case may be cited: a pre/post-fix source pair is public, although the Microblaze/GRC environment and exact build config are not. (2) 'EVS_AppID = CFE_EVS_UNDEF_APPID' is cited as the consistent-AppId-init fix, but that line already exists in pre-fix 6.4.1. It is the sentinel the issue complains about. The real EVS fix was setting EVS_AppID last in TaskInit, plus a range guard placed in EVS_SendEvent, not in EVS_IsFiltered. SB's AppId initialization never changed, and issue #2663 (2025) shows the SB/EVS ordering mismatch persists in modern cFE. (3) The fix first shipped in 6.4.2, per the VDD dated 2015-06-29 and the milestone. Citing 6.5.0a as where the fix lives is imprecise. The revision's 2015-05-06 and 2015-06-16 comment dates still could not be read directly, because comments were inaccessible. They are consistent with the 6.4.1 to 6.4.2 window, but that is an inference, not verification. That pattern fits a bulk migration of older tickets, but this is an inference. The issue describes a startup segfault found while debugging on the Microblaze processor used by the GRC EVA team. Core apps were created in the order EVS, SB, ES, TIME, TBL, with priorities 61, 64, 68, 60 and 70, so TIME ran first. TIME's CFE_SB_CreatePipe called CFE_EVS_SendEventWithAppID with the uninitialised CFE_SB.AppId (value 0). EVS was not yet initialised, so the call went through EVS_NotRegistered and EVS_SendEvent to EVS_IsFiltered with CFE_EVS_GlobalData.EVS_AppID = 0xFFFFFFFF. That value was not range-checked, and the access segfaulted. The out-of-range index was EVS's own sentinel, not SB's AppId.\n\nTwo of the three remedies proposed in the issue first appear publicly in cFE 6.4.2 (SourceForge tarball, listed 2015-07-13) and are kept in the 6.5.0a code on GitHub (792f5e35, b2765d9f):\n1. Each core app is started under CFE_ES_ApplicationSyncDelay, with CFE_PSP_Panic on timeout.\n2. A check that EVS_AppID < CFE_ES_MAX_APPLICATIONS was added, but in the caller EVS_SendEvent. EVS_IsFiltered itself is still unchecked.\n\nThe third remedy, consistent AppID values before initialisation, is not visible in the code. I confirmed this in the OCR text at a pinned GitHub mirror commit, and two search snippets of the same deck agree. The OCR loses the slide's arrows, so the direction of the relationship cannot be read from the layout. The slide title and the snippets do support 'listed as cFE heritage' (a predecessor), not 'runs cFE'. The date ordering only supports the narrower point that BAT's 2004 flight software was not built on cFE. The two framings also differ in substance: BAT's Triana-heritage CCSDS 'Software Bus' sits in the same GSFC lineage as cFE's Software Bus. Calling BAT 'unrelated to cFS' is therefore also an overreach.
